f our first meeting face to face,
nd the churchyard where, new-erected,
humble cross, in woodland gloom,
tands over my poor nurses tomb.
LVII
liss was so near, so altogether
ttainable!... But now my lot
s firmly cast. I dont know whether
acted thoughtlessly or not:
ou see, with tears and incantation
other implored me; my sad station
ade all fates look the same... and so
married. I beseech you, go;
know your heart: it has a feeling
or honour, a straightforward pride.
love you whats the use to hide
ehind deceit or double-dealing?
ut Ive become anothers wife --
nd Ill be true to him, for life.
LVIII
he went -- and Eugene, all emotion,
tood thunder-struck. In what wild round
f tempests, in what raging ocean
is heart was plunged! A sudden sound,
he clink of rowels, met his hearing;
atyanas husband, now appearing...
ut from the hero of my tale,
ust at this crisis of his gale,
eader, we must be separating,
or long... for evermore. Weve chased
im far enough through wild and waste.
urrah! lets start congratulating
urselves on our landfall. Its true,
ur vessels long been overdue.
LIX
eader, I wish that, as we parted --
hoever you may be, a friend,
foe -- our mood should be warm-hearted.
oodbye, for now we make an end.
hatever in this rough confection
ou sought -- tumultuous recollection,
rest from toil and all its aches,
r just grammatical mistakes,
vivid brush, a witty rattle --
od grant that from this little book
or hearts delight, or fun, you took,
or dreams, or journalistic battle,
od grant you took at least a grain.
n this well part; goodbye again!
And my companion, so mysterious,
oodbye to you, my true ideal,
y task, so vivid and so serious
nd yet so light. All that is real
nd enviable for a poet,
n your pursuit Ive come to know it:
blivion of lifes stormy ways,
weet talk with friends. How many days
ince, through the mist that dreams arise on,
oung Tanya first appeared to me,
negin too -- and there to see,
free romances far horizon,
till dim, through crystals magic glass,
efore my gaze began to pass.
I
f those who heard my opening pages
n friendly gatherings where I read,
s Sadi sang in earlier ages,
ome are far distant, some are dead.
heyve missed Eugenes completed etching.
ut she who modelled for the sketching
f Tanyas image... Ah, how great
he toll of those borne off by fate!
lest he whos left the hurly-burly
f lifes repast betimes, nor sought
o drain its beaker down, nor thought
f finishing its book, but early
as wished it an abrupt goodbye --
nd, with my Eugene, so have I.
otes to Chapter Eight
Gavrila  Derzhavin  1745-1816, Russias first outstanding  poet
abokov. While still at the Lyceum in Tsarskoe Selo, in 1815, Pushkin read
ome of his verses to him. The stanza was unfinished.
Lenore, romantic ballad by Gottfried August Burger, 1773.
Rout Eng.,  an evening  assembly without dancing;  means properly
rowd. Pushkins note.
Refers to Pushkins poem The Demon, of 1823.
Hero of Griboedovs Woe from Wit, 1824.
Admiral Alexander Shishkov 1754-1841  championed the purity ot  the
ussian language against the encroachment of foreign words.
Probably an allusion to Bulgarin,  an unfriendly  critic of Pushkins
ork.
Nina Voronskoy, imaginary belle of Petersburg society.
Court  decoration given  to  the Empresss ladies-in-waiting.  Stanza
nfinished.
